中华民族有着悠久的历史和灿烂的文化，系统、准确地

中国离不开世界，世界也缺少不了中国。中国文化摄取
外域的新成分，丰富了自己，又以自己的新成就输送给别
人，贡献于世界。从公元5世纪开始到公元15世纪，大约
有一干年，中国走在世界的前列。在这一干多年的时间里，
她的光辉照耀全世界。人类要前进，怎么能不全面认识中
国，怎么能不认真研究中国的历史呢？
中华民族是伟大的，曾经辉煌过，蓝天、白云、阳光灿
烂，和平而兴旺；也有过黑暗的、想起来就让人战栗的日
子，但中华民族从来是充满理想，不断追求，不断学习，渴
望和平与友谊的。
中国古代伟大的思想家孔子曾经说过：“三人行，必有
我师焉。择其善者而从之，其不善者而改之。”孔子的话就
是要人们向别人学习。这段话正是概括了整个中华民族与人
交往的原则。人与人之间交往如此，在与周边的国家交往中
也是如此。
秦始皇第一个统一了中国，可惜在位只有十几年，来不
及做更多的事情。汉朝继秦而继续强大，便开始走出去，了

是误传，但由此可见中外学者之间在中国长安交往的情谊。

大中华文库
此后的史籍对西人来华的记载，渐渐多了起来。13世纪
意大利旅行家马可·波罗，尽管有人对他是否真的到过中国
持怀疑态度，但他留下一部记述元代事件的《马可·波罗游
记》却是确凿无疑的。这部游记中的一些关于当时中国的描
述使得西方人认为是“天方夜谭”。总之，从中西文化交流
史来说，这以前的时期还是-一个想象和臆测的时代，相互之
间充满了好奇与幻想。
从16世纪末开始，由于航海技术的发展，东西方航路的
开通，随着一批批传教士来华，中国与西方开始了直接的交
流。沟通中西的使命在意大利传教士利玛窦那里有了充分的
体现。利玛窦于1582年来华，1610年病逝于北京，在华二
十余年。除了传教以外，做了两件具有历史象征意义的事，
一是1594年前后在韶州用拉丁文翻译《四书》，并作了注释；
二是与明代学者徐光启合作，用中文翻译了《几何原本》。
西方传教士对《四书》等中国经典的粗略翻译，以及杜
赫德的《中华帝国志》等书对中国的介绍，在西方读者的眼
前展现了一个异域文明，在当时及稍后一段时期弓起了一场
“中国热”，许多西方大思想家的眼光都曾注目于中国文化。
有的推崇中华文明，如莱布尼兹、伏尔泰、魁奈等，有的对
中华文明持批评态度，如孟德斯鸠、黑格尔等。莱布尼兹认
识到中国文化的某些思想与他的观念相近，如周易的卦象与
他发明的二进制相契合，对中国文化给予了热情的礼赞；黑
格尔则从他整个哲学体系的推演出发，认为中国没有真正意
义上的哲学，还处在哲学史前的状态。但是，不论是推崇还

今天，世界毕竟来到了21世纪的门槛。分散隔绝的世
界，逐渐变成联系为一体的世界。现在，全球一体化趋势日
益明显，人类历史也就在愈来愈大的程度上成为全世界的历
史。当今，任何一种文化的发展都离不开对其他优秀文化的
汲取，都以其他优秀文化的发展为前提。在近现代，西方文
化汲取中国文化，不仅是中国文化的传播，更是西方文化自
身的创新和发展；正如中国文化对西方文化的汲取一样，既
是西方文化在中国的传播，同时也是中国文化在近代的转型
和发展。地球上所有的人类文化，都是我们共同的宝贵遗
产。既然我们生活的各个大陆，在地球史上曾经是连成一气
的“泛大陆”，或者说是一个完整的“地球村”，那么，我们同
样可以在这个以知识和学习为特征的网络时代，走上相互学习、
共同发展的大路，建设和开拓我们人类崭新的“地球村”。
西学仍在东渐，中学也将西传。各国人民的优秀文化正
日益迅速地为中国文化所汲取，而无论西方和东方，也都需
要从中国文化中汲取养分。正是基于这一认识，我们组织出
版汉英对照版《大中华文库》，全面系统地翻译介绍中国传
统文化典籍。我们试图通过《大中华文库》，向全世界展示，
中华民族五干年的追求、五千年的梦想，正在新的历史时期
重放光芒。中国人民就像火后的凤凰，万众一心，迎接新世
纪文明的太阳。
東北京
1999年8月

all over the world. There have been many translations of the Chinese

China cannot be separated from the rest of the world; and the rest of
the world cannot ignore China. Throughout its history, Chinese civiliza-
tion has enriched itself by absorbing new elements from the outside world,
and in turn has contributed to the progress of world civilization as a whole
by transmitting to other peoples its own cultural achievements. From the
5th to the 15th centuries, China marched in the front ranks of world civi-
lization. If mankind wishes to advance, how can it afford to ignore China?
How can it afford not to make a thoroughgoing study of its history?
always been idealistic, and have never ceased to forge ahead and learn

an expedition which brought him within the confines of the Roman Empire.
Later, during the Tang Dynasty, the monk Xuan Zang made a journey
fraught with danger to reach India and seek the knowledge of that land.
Upon his return, he organized a team of scholars to translate the Buddhist
scriptures, which he had brought back with him. As a result, many of
these scriptural classics which were later lost in India have been pre-
served in China. In fact, it would have been difficult for the people of
India to reconstruct their own ancient history if it had not been for Xuan
Zang's A Record of a Journey to the West in the Time of the Great
Tang Dynasty. In the Ming Dynasty, Zheng He transmitted Chinese
culture to Southeast Asia during his seven voyages. Following the Opium
Wars in the mid-19th century, progressive Chinese, generation after gen-
eration, went to study the advanced scientific thought and cultural achieve-
ments of the Western countries. Their aim was to revive the fortunes of
leaders of China, including Zhu De, Zhou Enlai and Deng Xiaoping. In
addition, there were people who were to become leading scientists, liter-
ary figures and artists, such as Guo Moruo, Li Siguang, Qian Xuesen,
Xian Xinghai and Xu Beihong. Their spirit of ambition, their struggles and
their breadth of vision were an inspiration not only to the Chinese people
but to people all over the world.
Indeed, it is true that if the Chinese people had not learned many

# Page 16

things from the surrounding countries they would never have been able to
produce the splendid achievements of former days. When we look back

the civilizations of ancient Egypt, Greece and India? How can we not
feel fondness and respect for the cultures of Europe, Africa, America
and Oceania?
The Chinese nation, in turn, has made unique contributions to the com-
munity of mankind. Prior to the 15th century, China led the world in sci-
ence and technology. The British scientist Joseph Needham once said,
"From the third century A.D. to the 13th century A.D. China was far
ahead of the West in the level of its scientific knowledge.'" Paul Kennedy,
of Yale University in the U.S., author of The Rise and Fall of the Great
Powers, said, “"Of all the civilizations of the pre-modern period, none was
as well-developed or as progressive as that of China."
Foreigners who came to China were often astonished at what they
saw and heard. The Greek geographer Pausanias in the second century
A.D. gave the first account in the West of the technique of silk produc-
tion in China: “*The Chinese feed a spider-like insect with millet and reeds.
After five years the insect's stomach splits open, and silk is extracted
therefrom.'" From this extract, we can see that the Europeans at that
time did not know the art of silk manufacture. In the middle of the 9th
century A.D., an Arabian writer includes the following anecdote in his
Account of China and India:
"One day, an Arabian merchant called upon the military governor of
Guangzhou. Throughout the meeting, the visitor could not keep his eyes
off the governor's chest. Noticing this, the latter asked the Arab mer-
chant what he was staring at. The merchant replied, ‘Through the silk
robe must be made out of very fine silk indeed!' The governor burst out
laughing, and holding out his sleeve invited the merchant to count how
many garments he was wearing. The merchant did so, and discovered
that the governor was actually wearing five silk robes, one on top of the
other, and they were made of such fine material that a tiny mole could be
seen through them all! Moreover, the governor explained that the robes
he was wearing were not made of the finest silk at all; silk of the highest

# Page 17

grade was reserved for the garments worn by the provincial governor."

In this period, when Chinese culture was in full bloom, scholars flocked
from all over the world to China for sightseeing and for study. Chang'an,
the capital of the Tang Dynasty was host to visitors from as far away as
the Byzantine Empire, not to mention the neighboring countries of Asia.
Chang'an, at that time the world's greatest metropolis, was packed with
thousands of foreign dignitaries, students, diplomats, merchants, artisans
envoys to the Tang court. Worthy of note are the accounts of life in
in China and had close friendships with ministers of the Tang court and
many Chinese scholars in a period of over 50 years. The description
throws light on the exchanges between Chinese and foreigners in this
period. When Abeno was supposedly lost at sea on his way back home,
the leading poet of the time, Li Bai, wrote a eulogy for him.
The following centuries saw a steady increase in the accounts of China
written by Western visitors. The Italian Marco Polo described conditions
in China during the Yuan Dynasty in his Travels. However, until advances
in the science of navigation led to the opening of east-west shipping routes
at the beginning of the 16th century Sino-Western cultural exchanges
when a contingent of religious missionaries, men well versed in Western
science and technology, made their way to China, ushering in an era of

# Page 18

direct contacts between China and the West. The experience of this era
was embodied in the career of the Italian Jesuit Matteo Ricci. Arriving in
大中华
China in 1582, Ricci died in Beijing in 1610. Apart from his missionary
work, Ricci accomplished two historically symbolic tasks — one was the
文库
translation into Latin of the “Four Books,'" together with annotations, in
1594; the other was the translation into Chinese of Euclid's Elements.
The rough translations of the “Four Books'" and other Chinese classical
works by Western missionaries, and the publication of Pe re du Halde's
Description Geographique, Historique, Chronologique, Politique,
et Physique de l'Empire de la Chine revealed an exotic culture to West-
ern readers, and sparked a "China fever," during which the eyes of many
Western intellectuals were fixed on China. Some of these intellectuals,
including Leibniz, held China in high esteem; others, such as Hegel, nursed
a critical attitude toward Chinese culture. Leibniz considered that some
aspects of Chinese thought were close to his own views, such as the
philosophy of the Book of Changes and his own binary system. Hegel,
on the other hand, as mentioned above, considered that China had devel-
oped no proper philosophy of its own. Nevertheless, no matter whether
the reaction was one of admiration, criticism, acceptance or rejection,
Sino-Western exchanges were of great significance. The transmission of
advanced Chinese science and technology to the West, especially the
Chinese inventions of paper-making, gunpowder, printing and the compass,
greatly changed the face of the whole world. Karl Marx said, "Chinese
gunpowder blew the feudal class of knights to smithereens; the compass
opened up world markets and built colonies; and printing became an imple-
ment of Protestantism and the most powerful lever and necessary pre-
condition for intellectual development and creation." The English philoso-
pher Roger Bacon said that China's four great inventions had “changed
the face of the whole world and the state of affairs of everything."

ones whose influence has been felt right down to modern times, such as

Dunhuang Grottoes, Grand Canal and Great Wall. But from the latter
part of the 15th century, and for the next 4o0 years, Europe gradually
became the cultural centre upon which the world's eyes were fixed. The

Preface to the
The Chinese people then began to think: What is the cause of the rise
and fall of nations? Moreover, how did it happen that gunpowder, in-
Library of Chinese Classics
vented in China and transmitted to the West, in no time at all made Eu-
rope powerful enough to batter down the gates of China herself?
It took the Opium War to wake China from its reverie. The first gen-
eration to make the bold step of “turning our eyes once again to the rest
of the world'' was represented by Lin Zexu and Wei Yuan. Zeng Guofan
and Li Hongzhang started the Westernization Movement, and later intel-
lectuals raised the slogan of "Democracy and Science."" Noble-minded
patriots, realizing that China had fallen behind in the race for moderniza-
for change, and the quest produced the embryo of a towering hope, and
the Chinese people finally gathered under a banner proclaiming a “March
Toward Science."
On the threshold of the 21st century, the world is moving in the direc-
tion of becoming an integrated entity. This trend is becoming clearer by
the day. In fact, the history of the various peoples of the world is also
becoming the history of mankind as a whole. Today, it is impossible for
any nation's culture to develop without absorbing the excellent aspects of
the cultures of other peoples. When Western culture absorbs aspects of
Chinese culture, this is not just because it has come into contact with
Chinese culture, but also because of the active creativity and develop-
ment of Western culture itself; and vice versa. The various cultures of
the world's peoples are a precious heritage which we all share. Mankind

# Page 20

no longer lives on different continents, but on one big continent, or in a
"global village." And so, in this era characterized by an all-encompassing
network of knowledge and information we should learn from each other
大中华文库
and march in step along the highway of development to construct a brand-
new“global village."
Western learning is still being transmitted to the East, and vice versa.
China is accelerating its pace of absorption of the best parts of the cul-
tures of other countries, and there is no doubt that both the West and the
East need the nourishment of Chinese culture. Based on this recognition,
we have edited and published the Library of Chinese Classics in a Chi-
nese-English format as an introduction to the corpus of traditional Chi-
nese culture in a comprehensive and systematic translation. Through this
collection, our aim is to reveal to the world the aspirations and dreams of
the Chinese people over the past 5,oo0 years and the splendour of the
new historical era in China. Like a phoenix rising from the ashes, the
Chinese people in unison are welcoming the cultural sunrise of the new
century.
August 1999 Beijing

LIBRARYOFCHINESE
朗累官至议郎，迁尚书，又出为河内太守。汉灵帝建宁二年（公

《周易参同契》以道家思想为依托，广泛汲取先秦两汉的天文
学、医学、炼丹术以及易学方面的成就，借用《周易》符号系统
的结构框架，建构成以炼丹术为主体、天文历法为前提的天地人
三才合一的思想体系，对中国古代自然科学技术与人文思想的发
展产生过深远影响。
《契》在天文历法方面的主要思想
在天文学方面，《契》建构了一种“天地设位”“坎离匡郭”
的宇宙结构模型。其思想有取于浑天说的地内天外的浑天鸡子之
象，又将宣夜说充实进模型之中，以坎离二卦象征阴阳二气，坎
离毂转，即为阴阳二气上下浮沉、升降流行。坎离二卦为阴阳交

# Page 22

交错而成，坎离上下如同一年四季阴阳升降流行，即“阳往则阴
周易参同契
来，辐而轮转”。
前言
在历法方面，《契》本身并未形成独立的制历系统，它仅仅只
是依据炼丹的需要，采撷汉代易学中流行的象数模型如卦气说、
纳甲说、十二月消息卦、五行数图式等，以模拟炼丹时必须把握
的有关的历法问题。中国古代历法是阴阳历，它的基本要素是气
与朔。气是阳历成分，朔是阴历成分。《契》援引纳甲月相，偏重
讲朔，又辅以乾卦六交所象征的阳气升降，偏重讲气，体现－月
之内阴阳合历的特点。十二月消息卦，则象征着一年之内气候变
大中华文库
化的大致规律。
在天象观测方面，《契》特别关注日食现象。后汉四分历使用
平朔，尽管日食不一定都会在嗨朔二日发生，但毕竟在这两天内
发生的几率要高一些。《契》说“日月相薄食，常在晦朔间”，反
映的正是应用平朔的结果。嗨朔之间，即嗨至朔旦，是“天地
其精，日月相持”的日子，因而正是金丹炼制过程中阴阳交感，
雌雄错杂的产丹之时。如果这时出现日食，就被看作是天地阴阳
失调的结果，意味着金丹因火候失当而会失败，因此炼丹术对日
食现象的出现一直特别留意。
二《契》在易学史上的学术地位
《契》假借《周易》卦交之象，建构了众多天人模型，为道教
易学奠定了基本格局，对宋代兴起的先天学有着明显的影响，在
易学史上理应享有重要的地位。
《契》对汉代的象数易学无疑有一定程度的继承，但更具特色
的是它的独创性，主要表现在以下几个方面：
其一，用乾坤坎离四卦建构了－个宇宙结构模型。这个模型

在先秦与两汉尚无先例。它不仅揭示了宇宙结构的特征，而且提
Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi
出了坎离两卦是乾坤之二用的思想，大大凸显了坎离两卦的作用，

的基础理论产生了深远影响，宋代以后不少医家论述心肾二脏功能
之关系、肾与命门以及伤寒温热之辨，大都援坎离水火以为说。
其二，用《易》的天地之数图（即五行生成数图，亦即宋代
CLASSICS
以后所说的河图）建构了一种天人模型。这个模型以河图外圈的
LIBRARYOFCHINESE
八七九六这四个成数象征天道阴阳，其中七为少阳，八为少阴，
九为老阳，六为老阴。所谓“九还七返，八归六居”，描述的是天
道阴阳的升降变化。又用内圈的一二三四五这五个生数象征人体
或炉火，说明内丹或外丹的炼制过程。在人体，一为肾水黑，二
为心火赤，三为肝木青，四为肺金白，五为脾土黄。在炉火，一
为汞，四为铅，二为炉火，三为木炭，五为木炭燃尽后所成之土。
这只是其静态配置，而在其动态运作中，这些数字都是一种模型
语言，要具体结合其描述的对象才能理解。这个模型不用河图数
中的成数十，与《黄帝内经》中七篇大论所说的运气学说取数相
同。可能也是因为“土常以生”（《素问·天元正纪大论》），所以
土只用生数五，而不用成数十。
其三，在纳甲法的基础上建构了月相纳甲模型。这个模型的
主要内容是：初三月初生，以震卦象之，纳庚；初八月相上弦，
以兑卦象之，纳丁；十五月望，以乾卦象之，纳甲王。十六以后，
月相由望转缺，以巽卦象之，纳辛；二十三月相下弦，以艮卦象
之，纳丙；三十月晦，以坤卦象之，纳乙癸。坎为月，纳戊；离为
日，纳己。戊已为土，居模型中宫之位，月相的晦朔弦望，皆因
日月之动而成。后汉末年，虞翻全盘继承了《契》中月相纳甲法，

# Page 24

将其作为解《易》的基本条例之一。由此可见，月相纳甲模型在
周易参同契前言
汉代易学中的地位。此外，月相纳甲中坎离之外六卦的位次，若
以乾顺坤逆之意铺排，并填人坎离，一幅先天八卦次序图就形成
了。《契》是否有此意未可得知，而其与先天八卦次序的近似，已
将二者之间的关系客观地呈现出来。朱熹说：“邵子发明先天图，
图传自希夷，希夷又自有所传，盖方士技术用以修炼，《参同契》
所言是也。”（朱熹《周易参同契考异》卷首附)
三《契》在炉火炼制方面的成就
大中华文库
《契》在金丹炼制方面所取得的成就，在化学史与医药史上享
有重要地位。《契》在金丹炼制上主要涉及黄丹、灵丹与玄黄丹，
这三种丹品位各不相同，投人的原料、炼制过程与反应的具体情
况也不相同。
1．黄丹，又称铅丹（PbO4），实际上是一种铅酸铅盐
（Pb2PbOa），其色鲜艳橘红。《契》载炼制黄丹的主要原料为胡粉，
即碳酸铅（PbCO，），色白，又称铅粉、水粉。炼制过程大致分为
两步，首先提纯真铅，然后进行反复炒炼，使之成丹。
《契》云“胡粉投火中，色坏还为铅”，胡粉在火中受热分解释
放出二氧化碳，生成一氧化铅（即密陀僧，其色黄）。反应式为：
PbCO, + C + O2 -→PbO + 2CO2
继续加热，一氧化铅进一步与木火之碳和空气中之氧反应，
生成铅。反应式为：
经过提炼的真铅，又称为金华、金精。将真铅捣成碎粒，然
后在炒锅中加热炒炼，即可制成黄丹。《契》后所附《大丹赋》所
云“先白而后黄兮，赤黑通表里”，是说真铅在炒锅内随氧化程度

2．灵丹（HgS），又称灵砂、金砂、丹砂，由自然生成的丹砂
经反复炼制而成。《契》炼制灵丹的方法，首先将丹砂矿石捣碎，
置入反应器中，然后务必将反应器密固坚实。加温时开始用文火，
促使HgS矿石缓慢熔化分解为水银（Hg）与硫磺（S），等到熔化
CLASSICS
分解到一定程度，再用武火确保其完全充分分解，即所谓“终竟
LIBRARY OF CHINESE
武乃陈”。其反应式为：
580℃
然后抽薪降温，令其冷却，使分解出来的Hg和S蒸气在升炼
装置中重新合成，再度凝结成HgS。其反应式为：
硫化汞（HgS）的分解与化合，在鼎器内要反复进行十二次，
即“周旋十二节”。经过十二节的周旋，到出炉之时，反应器中分
解出来的水银和硫磺都已不复独立存在，即所谓“气索命将绝，
休死亡魂魄”。二者已经化合，灵丹即在这种反复的分解与化合的
过程中炼成，即“色转更为紫，赫然成还丹”。
3．玄黄丹，又称龙虎大还丹。《契》有关玄黄丹的炼制有两
种配料：其一，以黄丹与灵丹合炼；其二，以真铅与真汞合炼。
现分述如下：
以黄丹与灵丹合炼，则前述两种炼丹过程皆属于采药。黄丹
与灵丹合炼的反应式为：
3Pb,O4 + HgS —→HgO + 9PbO + SO2
SO2挥发后，余下的HgO与PhO为混合物，色黄。这种氧化

# Page 26

汞和一氧化铅（密陀僧）的混合物，就是术士们心目中的玄黄，
■周易参同契前言
即龙虎大还丹.
第二种配方是真铅与真汞，合炼前先当提纯铅和汞，上述反
应式（2）可提纯铅，反应式（5）可提纯汞
《契》对提纯后真铅与真汞的合炼作过说明，大意是令铅布鼎
之四壁，进火时先得温而熔（铅的熔点328℃，Hg的沸点357℃），
待铅全部熔化后就抽薪退符，使铅汞混合物凝聚，结成黄舆即玄
黄金丹，色黄如金。这种金丹的化学成分，现在称之为Pb一Hg
齐。《契》认为此丹一符即可，久炼则毁性伤丹，对人有害。
大中华文库
玄黄金丹，即龙虎大还丹。，龙象征灵丹或真汞，虎象征黄丹
或真铅，取象于二十八宿中的东西二象，以比拟两种药料在鼎炉
中互相吸取的炼制状况。根据《契》“旁有垣阙”的描述，炼制两
种不同配方的玄黄丹，使用的鼎炉是相同的。
在黄丹、灵丹、玄黄三种丹中，玄黄是上品仙丹，是“道成
德就”的最高追求，灵丹次之为中品，黄丹又次之为下品。从药
用价值上说，黄丹可以外敷杀菌而不可内服，灵丹可以配药内服，
随佐使而见其功。唯有上品之尊的玄黄，既无外敷之用，更无内
服之功，服食者唯仙解一途而已。从修们的意义上说，金丹的全
部炼制都是失败的，但从医药史、化学史、治金史上看，金丹的
炼制成果却具有不可磨灭的历史意义。黄丹与灵丹是最早由人工
制成的具有药用价值的化学制品。自汉代以来，一直为医家所注
重，因而不仅在中药学发展过程中产生过重要作用，尤其在世界
化学药品的制造和使用时限上堪称一最，故理应在世界药物学史
上享有崇高的历史地位。在化学与冶金方面，《契》成功地描述了
铅的氧化还原反应、硫化汞的分解与化合反应、铅汞齐的互化反
应，无论在化学史还是在冶金工艺史上，从时限说在世界范围内
都是绝无仅有的。

# Page 27

Preface
Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi
四《契》在内丹养生方面的成就
在养生方面，《契》对中国古代调气养身的学说进行了全面系
统的总结与发展，规范了修炼的基本方法，提出了循序渐进的修
炼步骤，从而将古代人体科学的发展推进到了一个新的水平。
《契》认为：“人所禀躯，体本一无，元精流布，因气托初。”
人体之初，是无形的阴阳之气交感造化的产物。由此形成的人体，
CLASSICS
是一个魂与魄“互为室宅”、性与情“恒为城郭”的和谐整体，与
LIBRARY OF CHINESE
天地阴阳的和谐统一是一致的，即“情和乾坤”，说明人体的自然
特性与天地的运行节律是相应的。为此，《契》提出一套循序渐进
的修炼方法与步骤，以实现成神成仙的愿望。
第一步，谓之“闭塞其兑，筑固灵株”。这一步属于炼丹前的
自身调理，目标是祛除病魔，补足亏损。方法是通过养性敛神，
使人体阴阳之动与天道阴阳之升降合节。《契》说：“内以养已，
安静虚无，原本隐明，内照形躯，闭塞其兑，筑固灵株。”己，在
月相纳甲图式中为离中己土，即意念，为性根之所寄，故养己在
于养神。“筑固灵株”，也就是要使心灵处于安静虚无之境，无为
无念，无思无欲。内照，即眼附于神，内视自身形躯之阴阳升降
运行。兑，为口，闭口不言则上不泄，筑固灵株则下不漏。炼已
有成，则觉一气流通，和顺适中而英华外发，即《契》所谓“黄
中渐通理，润泽达肌肤”。
第二步，谓之“知白守黑”，运转“河车”。依五行配五脏之
例，用河图五行数图式，《契》建构了一个人体五脏模型。在这个
模型中：水数一，配五脏为肾，其色黑；火数二，配五脏为心，其
色赤；木数三，配五脏为肝，其色青；金数四，配五脏为肺，其色
白；土数五，配五脏为脾，其色黄。这是一个人体五脏的静态模

# Page 28

型，其中的一、二、三、四、五，是河图内圈的生数方位。这个模
周易参同契
型的动态运作的调控中心为中宫戊已，脾土所在之位，主意念。
《契》所谓“知白守黑”讲的是运转河车功法。运转河车为运气，
通三关（尾闾、夹脊、玉枕），至于泥丸穴。运转河车功法，又称
引一进四，如进火。气感为“若有若无”，“乍沉乍浮”。气至泥
丸，则与心火相交。火赤数二，“火性销金，金伐木荣”。肝木青，
数三。此为炼二归三，即气沿任脉而下人丹田，如退符。如此往
返升降，全赖中五戊己脾土以意引导。一四合为一五，二三又为
大中华文库
一五，中五自成一五。故《契》云“三五为一，天地至精”，又云
“三物一家，都归戊己”。后世称这一功法为小周天。
第三步，谓之“徘徊子与午”的“两孔穴法”。小周天功法炼
到一定火候，便可进人第三步。相对第三步而言，小周天功法只
是采药归炉，第三步才实现了将归炉之药温养凝结为丹。大周天
功法不走任督二脉，而是内视元气氮盒于泥丸、腹脐、尾间之间。
五行模型中，为北水一、南火二、戊已土五，一条直线。由于此
功法运内气于泥丸尾间之间，则子在北可以之南，午在南可以之
北，颠倒相就，故云“子南午北，互为纲纪”。从这个意义上说，
大周天功法又可称为活子午法。此法犹天道阴阳之上下浮沉，即
如同“天地之雌雄，徘徊子与午”。此功法之所以被称为“两孔穴
法”，是从坎离两卦的卦象上取义的，即言坎离两用之窍妙。坎肾
水、离心火、中脾土，是此功法中的三个要素，肾水心火之升降，
关键靠牌土意念调平胜负。
第四步，谓之“委志归虚无，无念以为常”的闭关修炼法。
此法是炼丹的最高阶段。从功法上看，这一步似乎又回到开始的
“筑基”阶段，这就是所谓的“反者道之验”。这一步的要求是，
人之外三宝（耳、目、口）要做到“闭塞勿发扬”。只是内视二气

在体内周流，使内三宝（精、气、神）不外泄。杜绝房事，无思

无念，意无须领气，亦不可纵横驰骋。寝寐时形神相抱，觉醒时
静待阳存阴亡，即“排除众阴邪，然后立正阳”。炼此功法，初时
昏昏然，一切皆在恍惚之间。继以时日，其和气逐渐周匝于一身，
往来上下，百脉冲融，畅于四肢。这一阶段成果，是术士们苦苦
追求的最终目标。

说成神成仙之类的追求如何荒诞，如何愚不可及，但却不可否认，
CLASSICS
《契》所总结的调气养身之术，是人类在与疾病斗争和征服衰老过
LIBRARY OFCHINESE
程中所取得的不可磨灭的伟大成就。正是由于《契》在炼丹史奠
定的历史地位和在中国文化特定层面上的重大影响，《契》一直被
后世尊为“丹经之祖”或“万古丹经王”。
五《契》的流传状况
在《契》的流传过程中，由于其所涵盖的知识面之宽广与其
内蕴之精深，导致它在不同历史时期仅仅只以某一层面的光芒显
现于世。在魏晋时期，注《契》者看重的是它的易学，故葛洪有
“儒者不知神仙之事，多作阴阳注之”之说（《云笈七·神仙
传·魏伯阳》）。在隋唐，注《契》者看重的是其中的炉火金丹术，
今存阴长生注、容字号无名氏注、彭晓注是其证。宋元明清，注
《契》者多重其调气养生术，今存者如宋代储华谷、陈显微、朱
熹，元代俞琰、陈致虚，明代徐渭、陆西星、李文烛、甄淑、张
位、王文录、杜一诚、王九灵、蒋一彪，清代李光地、仇兆鳌、刘
一明、刘吴龙、朱元育、董德宁、陶素帮、姜中真、尹太铉等人之
注是其证。另内外丹兼重的也不乏其人，如隋代青霞子《读金碧
龙虎经》。《契》既言外丹亦言内丹，明代彭好古《古文参同契》

# Page 30

■周易参回
三卷，在版本上推崇古文本，而在内容上则内外兼重。《契》并无
外丹、内丹之称，后世为分别见，以《契》中之“炉火服食”为
外丹，另称其中“引内养性”之说为内丹。
《契》在长期流传过程中出现了简策错乱以及篇章结构的混乱
现象，由此引出该书的作者归属问题。葛洪《神仙传·魏伯阳》
称“伯阳作《参同契》《五相类》凡二卷”，这应当是魏伯阳作
《契》的本来状况，即《参同契》《五相类》各一卷。至东晋时
期，陶弘景《真浩》卷十二自注云：“《易参同契》云：桓帝时，
上虞淳于叔通，受述于青州徐从事，仰观乾象，以处灾异，数有
大中华文库
效验。以知术故，郡举方十，迁洛阳市长。”余嘉锡先生认定此段
引文是“《参同契》序中之文”，是一个可信的判断。此序只交代
了淳于叔通与徐从事的师承关系，并无一字提到此二人是否与作
《契》或传《契》有关联，后世或猜测此二人是传《契》之人，
或猜测是《契》中某一部分的作者，原因或与《鼎器歌》（又称
《龙虎之歌》）、《大丹赋》被附于正文有关，新旧《唐志》载《五
相类》仍为一卷，而《参同契》则载为二卷，比葛洪所见本多出
一卷，应是此二篇附人的结果，而淳于叔通、徐从事进人《契》
学系统也应当与此二篇的著作权有关，唐叶期以后，主要流行的
是将五言句（即《参同契》）与四言句（即《五相类》）打散编为
上、中两篇（或上、中两卷），又将四言句结语与后序，以及《鼎
器歌》《大丹赋》混编为下篇（或下卷）。现存的阴长生本与彭晓
本大致反映了这种上中下三篇（或三卷）本的面貌。明代正德年
间，出现了杜一诚编定的古本《周易参同契》，以四言句为经，五
言句为传，其余为《三相类》，共三册，每册各附以原序。嘉靖年
间杨慎托名得石函古本《参同契》，并大力加以提倡，由是古本
《契》身价倍增。以后蒋一彪、李光地、仇兆鳌、袁仁林、刘一
明、刘吴龙等皆采用古本，并在篇章结构与作者归属方面有不同

及《周易参同契》。这些译本基本上把《契》看作是一本有关内丹

早的英译本。笔者在汉语今译中，除了在前言中对《契》中炉火

译本之不足。由于英译本与汉语今译在时间上相距七八十年，在

大中华文库
一致，这是需要读者谅解的。

I . Major Thoughts of Astronomy and
Calendar in Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi
From the perspective of astronomy, Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi establi-
shes a universe structure model of “ heaven and earth in respective posi-
tions' and “ Kan and Li being likened in configuration to the four walls
of a city'. Such thoughts originate from the theory of sphere-heavens,
according to which the universe is shaped like a hen's egg with the
earth in the center shrouded by the heaven. The structure model is fur-
ance in the night sky, with the two hexagrams of Kan and Li symboli-
zing the yin and yang energies and the alternation of the two hexagrams
symbolizing the rise and fall of the two energies. The two hexagrams of
Kan and Li are formed by the different formations of the whole and bro-
ken linear symbols, and the alternation of these two symbolizes the wax
and wane of the yin and yang in the four seasons of a year, namely
" When yang is gone, yin will come on to take its place. A wheel turns

Zhou Yi
mirrored in the existing versions compiled by Yin Changsheng and Peng

CLASSICS

sion was thus increased significantly. Since then, Jiang Yibiao of the

thorship of the book. The efforts described above indicate that , despite
a number of errors, the true origin of the book was restored to some ex-
tent.
I have done some studies on the Qi and have written a monograph
( The Study of Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi, published by Shanghai Cultural
Publishing House in January 2oo1 ) , of which the second part is an exe-
gesis on the book. In my exegesis , the Yin Changsheng version of the
Tang dynasty, the earliest one available up to now, is adopted as the
master copy. Meanwhile, it refers to the existing notes, commentaries
and quoted texts of the Tang and Song dynasties and sometimes those of
the Ming and Qing dynasties, for emendation of the version. As the
structure is based on the ancient version, the five-character verses are
adopted for the Can Tong Qi part , the four-character verses for Catego-
ry of Five Phases. The prefaces of these two sections are proven to

# Page 2

周易参同
have been written by Wei Boyang, so they are included in the book;
同契
however, as Song of the Ding and Ode to the Immortal Pills were prob-
前言
ably created by Xu Congshi and Chunyu Shutong, they are therefore
excluded from the main body but attached to the book as the appendi-
ces. The prose passages in the body of the book might be exegesis
made by later exegetes, so they are typeset in smaller fonts. In addi-
tion , the order of and transition between the paragraphs are regularized
on the basis of the meaning from the context and rhymes of particular
verses. Wei Boyang observes that Category of Five Phases is the sup-
大中华文库
plement to the Qi to make up for omissions and to further explain the
profound theories, so the general ideas of these two sections are com-
pletely identical. By comparing the four-character verses and the five-
character verses, we find that the former does play the role of further
interpreting the latter; therefore, the five-character verses are included
in the Qi and the four-character ones in Category of Five Phases, which
means the current version of the book turns out to be quite similar to the
original version.
This modern Chinese version is based on Yu Yan's Review of
"Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi" of the Yuan dynasty, so as to be consistent
with the English translation version of the master copy. As a result, the
undesirable arrangement of the paragraphs and sections of the book still
remains, and I have to, based on the same version by Yu Yan, finish
the Chinese translation of the book with a little reluctance, and basical-
ly form an integral work.
With respect to those English translations of Zhou Yi Can Tong
Qi, the earliest version was jointly created by Wu Luqiang and Tenney
L. Davis and published in the 18th issue of Isis in 1932, in pages 210

-289. Moreover, Zhou Shiyi's English translation of the book was

Tong
plete or partial English translations by foreign scholars include Richard
Bertsingh's The Secret of the Future-the First Translation of Chinese
Classics on the Immortals published in 1994 by Element Press in Locke-
port, Massachusetts, USA; Huang Aiwa's translation of Doctrine of
the Tao, published in 1997 by Shambhala Publications in Boston, in
which Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi was introduced in pages 80 - 86. In all of
CLASSICS
these translations, Zhou Yi Can Tong Qi is generally deemed as a book
LIBRARY OF CHIN
on the inner alchemy instead of the outer alchemy. Among these trans-
lation versions , the one by Wu Luqiang and Tenney L. Davis interprets
well the phase-maths model and the theory of the masculine, the femi-
nine and the Five Elements of the Book of Change. Therefore, this ear-
liest English translation is adopted in this book. My modern Chinese
translation describes in detail the achievements of the outer alchemy in
the preface. The modern Chinese translation is intended to be supple-
mentary to the English translation. However, as there is a long time-
span of 70 - 80 years between the adopted English translation and the
modern Chinese translation, it shall be known to the readers that full
conformity of the two translation versions is impossible.
Xiao Hanming
June, 2009
Wuchang
( Translated into English by Bai Chongshun)

上篇
乾坤者，《易》之门户，众卦之父母讠
坎离匡郭，运毂正轴。
大中华文库
牡牡四卦，以为篱②
在《易》六十四卦中，乾坤两卦最为重要。这两卦是《易》
的门户，也是生成其他众卦的父母。
乾坤两卦象征天地，坎离两卦象征日月。如车之用必先正其
轴，然后贯入毂中才能运转一样，日月必定围绕着某个中轴，才
能循环运行于天地之间，其所成之轨迹如同一个周匝的城郭之形。
乾，纯阳之卦，为牡；坤，纯阴之卦，为靴；坎，阴中有阳，
为靴；离，阳中有阴，为牡。故所谓北牡四卦，即指此乾、坤、
坎、离四卦。此四卦，可以建构成一个宇宙模型。

CLASSICS
Yang (male, positive) is like the work of a skilled driver, holding the

LIBRARY OF CHINESE

朔旦屯直事，至暮蒙当受，

上篇
既未至昧爽，终则复更始。
日辰为期度?，动静有早晚。
春夏据内体，从子到辰已；
秋冬当外用，自午讫戌亥。
赏罚应春秋°，昏明顺寒暑，
交辞有仁义，随时发喜怒。
如是应四时，五行得其序?
大中华文库
六十卦值日之次序，依《序卦传》之序（即通行本之卦序），
昼夜各用一卦，起于朔日，朝为屯卦，暮为蒙卦。
自此相继为朝需卦暮讼卦、朝师卦暮主比卦，依次而
用，直至晦日朝为既济卦暮为未济卦，循环往复，终而复始。
一日有十二个时辰，人在早晚的动静应与时辰相吻合，如日
出而作，日入而息，即应做到与天度相合。
一年之内，春夏两季之气候变化为阳气渐升，人应当以此为
据，蓄养自身之阳气；在一天之内，从子时到辰巳之时，相当于
一年之春夏，也不要使阳气外泄。
秋冬两季为阴气渐升，在一天之内，从午时至戌亥之时也与
此相类，应防御外界阴气的侵袭。
行赏与用罚应当与春秋季节的阴阳二气相适应。春天万物发
生，赏有功以助阳气之上扬；秋天万物凋零，罚有罪以顺阴气之
肃杀。一日之昏与明，顺从于一年之寒暑，日出为明而气渐温，
日入为昏而气转凉。
交辞涵有仁义之理，阳刚为仁，阴柔为义，吉凶悔吝之类的
判词会引起人或喜或怒的情感变化，而判词又是对交辞所反映的
时势的判定，故人之喜怒实际上是随时势的变化而发生的。
由此可见，人事的变化与四时是相应的，五行因此而有确定
的次序c

天地者，乾坤之象也；
设位者?，列阴阳配合之位也，《易》谓坎离。
坎离者，乾坤二用。
二用无交位?，周流行六虚，往来既不定，上下亦无常。
幽潜沦匿，变化于中，

大中华文库
故推消息，坎离没亡
乾卦象征天，坤卦象征地，是天地为乾坤之象。
所谓“设位”，就是以离卦象征日，坎卦象征月，日月运行于
天地之间，以东西为出入之门，象征阴阳二气升降之位。
在这个模型中，乾坤二卦是其体，而坎离二卦则是乾坤之功用。
二用在卦中没有固定的交位，周流升降于卦的六个交位之间，
往来既无定所，上下也没有常规。
阴阳二气幽深潜藏而莫测，混沦隐匿而难分，万物的变化即
存于其中。
这个混沦之体包裹着万物，是万物发生的根源，因而是道的
纲要与关键所在。
这个混沦之体无象无形，却制约着有形万物的生成与发展。
器物之所以有其功用，是由于器内有空间。
所以要推究阴阳二气的升降变化的根源，正在于坎离二卦隐
没不可见的晦朔之时。

After the heavens and the earth have provided the proper
CLASSICS
positions, Yi (Change） operates among them. To have provided the
positions is to have placed yin and yang ( female and male, negative
LIBRARY OF CHINESE
and positive) in matching positions. Yi (Change) refers to the Kan and
Li guas. They embody the qualities of Qian and Kun. The latter two do
not have any fixed positions. They flow about in the Six Vacua ( 六
虚). They come and go without any regularity, and even so do they
fluctuate up and down. Rising and falling all the while, they sometimes
hide themselves away or vanish for a short time. They embrace all
creation and are the fundamentals of the Dao ( Way). In order to
secure emptiness, many tangible things are made. Vessels are useful

月相纳甲之言词不是随意编造的，其理论也不是凭空产生的。
引用验证可以见其效果的正确无误，通过校正测度可见其神
明的功能。
依事物的类别相推来造字，是以探求事物之间的道理为依
坎纳戊，为月之精；离纳己，为日之光。
日上月下就构成了一个“易”字，日为昼为刚，月为夜为柔，
故“易”字体现了刚柔相当之理。
在月相纳甲图式中，坎戊离己为土居中位。春夏秋冬四季都
离不开土的作用。
春为青为木，位在东方；夏为赤为火，位在南方；秋为白为
金，位在西方；冬为黑为水，位在北方。四季在图式中各居一方。
四季的交替，全凭居于戊己中宫的日月升降所发挥的功用。

大中华文库
了初始之义。
复卦初交一阳自乾来复，下体成震，乾为父，震为长男，震
一阳来下，以继承乾父之体。上体坤为母，下体之长男震乃乾之
一阳来交坤之初交而生，故震因坤母而立其开始之根基。
卦交的阴阳消息与十二月钟律变换相适应，确定阴阳交升降
的依据是北斗星斗柄在十二个月内的不同指向。
月初三日，一芽新月清晰生出，其象如震卦一阳下生之形。
在月相纳甲模型中，震纳庚，位在西方。
初八日，月上弦，平展如绳，其象如兑卦二阳在下之形。在
模型中，兑纳丁，位在南方。
月十五日，月相通圆明亮，其象如乾卦之三阳已然成就，兴
盛圆满地冠甲于东方。在模型中，乾纳甲，位在东方。
十五日，日月相望。日现于昼，月出于夜，两者不会同时明
照于天空。蟾蜍与兔魄，是月中之精物，视着卦交的节律性变
化，并在月十五日吐出满轮月光。
七八之和为一十五日，上半月之日数已毕，下半月之月光便
会由满月而逐渐下降。
月十六日，转而开始了这种下降的趋势，月之下体出现微缺，
于平明之时现于东南方。其象如巽卦之一阴在下。在模型中，巽
纳辛，位在东南。
月二十三日，月下弦。其象如艮卦之形。模型中，艮纳丙，
位在南方。
月至三十日为嗨，其象如坤卦三阴全布，明月之光完全丧失。
模型中，坤纳乙，位在东北。
将一个月分为六节，六节过尽，下一月之六节相与禅替，接
续而至的便又是震卦，其象为龙。

于洞虚，好似消亡一般。
在此之时，谬误往往在于失却事绪，言谈必然导致自取伤败。

秘，不至于盲无头绪。

可能使其合体而成金丹呢？
如此举措千次必定会失败万次，欲以點慧炼制，反成痴愚，
至终也未能侥幸得遇成功。圣人独知其谬，而昧者难以自明。
自稚齿之年至白首之岁，中途产生狐疑，
背离大道，困守迷途，由正道而出，入于邪门歪道。
此等之人，以管窥天，见识不广，何以度方来无穷之奥妙。

夏至、冬至若改变了常度，气候就会出现乖异错乱与曲折辗
转的现象。
在隆冬大寒的季节出现了大暑炎热的天气，在盛夏暑热的季
节出现了霜雪冻冽之严寒。
春分、秋分若纵横颠倒，与昼夜刻漏相均之常度不相应，
风雨便会不应节令而至，水与旱有如兵戈交相克伐，旱涝相继，
蝗虫如泉水之涌沸，山岳大地无故崩裂。
天显现其怪象，众多奇异之事层出不穷。
孝子专心志诚于孝敬父母，可以感动皇天上帝。
言谈近出于己之口，而声教则远远流布于殊域。
或因其言不善而招致祸患，或因其言善而得福，
或因其言兴太平之世，或因其言而导致兵革纷争，
此四者之所以到来，都出于心胸一念之间。

之兆端渐渐滋益而彰著。

相趋。正月配地支为寅，配音律为太簇。

九还于西，七返于南，八归于东，唯有六静居于北，此为五
行之数在交購之后回复本位，各守境域之象。
男之精液色白，女之经血色赤。白为金之色，赤为火之色。
精血交融，即为金与火相互拘取。

最善良的品德好像水一样，清澈而没有瑕疵，与道相似。
道没有形象，其真一之气的神妙作用难以用画图的方式以示人。
其变化所生出之五行，分布于东南西北中五方，各自独居其位。

星为河魁，魁配地支属戌，天干为辛。辛金值秋，有肃杀之气，
所以二月而榆荚落。
八月万物至秋而遭肃杀，而麦类却于此时发芽生长。八月之
将星为天罡，天罡配地支属辰，天干为乙，乙木值春，有生长之
气，所以八月而麦生。

方水位而居下，其气上升而自子向南；午在南方火位而居上，其
气下降而自午往北。二者往来迭运，上下变迁，互为关键性依据。

我忧虑与悯恤那些喜好道术的后生之辈，担心他们误入歧途，
于是依傍道术之风采，指画《火记》一类古文，著成图书典籍，
为后辈开启其修炼之孔窍，明示其入道之门户。
然而由于其义深奥，我不敢泄露天机，只能显露其枝条，将
其本根隐藏于言语之外。
后人不知古人之妙旨，托其名号于众多矿石之中，反复妄为
著述，错谬古文。
学者得到这类著述，不识真假，以为至宝，终身蕴藏之于柜
匣之中，子继承其父之业，孙又接踵其祖父之先志，
子孙相传，世代迷惑不解，对正道无所见闻。
以至仕宦者辞去官职，农夫废弃田园不事耕耘，经商之人放
弃货殖，有志之士家道贫困潦倒。
我对这些现象甚为伤感，所以决定录制此篇文章，

O, the sages of old! They held in their bosoms the elements of
CLASSICS
profundity and truth. Having compounded and partaken of the medicines
LIBRARY OFCHINESE
prepared in the nine ding (furnaces ), they were endowed with the
power to disappear at will. They held the essence firmly and cultivated
their spirit, and they thereby attained communion with the three
primes. The essential fluids worked properly to give them fine, strong
bones and muscles. With the various evils banished, the righteous
[good] qi (ethereal essence） came to stay with them forever. In
135
course of time they became immortals.
Their sympathy for those of posterity , who might have a liking for
the attainment of the Dao (Way) , led them to explain the writings of
old with words and illustrations. They couched their ideas in the names
of stones and in vague language so that only some branches, as it were ,
were in view and the roots were securely hidden. Those who had access
to the discourses wasted their own lives over them. The same path of
misery was followed by one generation after another with the same
failure. If an official, his career was cut short; if a farmer, his farm
was cluttered with weeds; if a merchant, his trade was abandoned; if
an ambitious scholar , his family became destitute-in the vain attempt.
These grieve me and have prompted the present writing.  Although

《参同契》者，敷陈梗概，不能纯一，泛滥而说，纤微未备，阔
略仿佛。今更撰录，补塞遗脱，润色幽深，钩援相逮。旨意等齐，
所趣不悖，故复作此，命《五相类》。则大《易》之情性尽矣。
大易情性，各如其度。黄老用究，较而可御。炉火之事，真
有所据。三道由一，俱出径路。
枝茎华叶，果实垂布。正在根株，不失其素。诚心所言，审
而不误。
大中华文库
国鄙夫，幽谷朽生。挟怀朴素，不乐权荣。栖迟僻陋，忽
略利名。执守恬淡，希时安平。晏然闲居，乃撰斯文。
《参同契》，只是对丹道做了一个大略的铺叙，由于担心泄露
玄机，所以述说中多借事隐喻，广为之叙说，未能做到纯一而不
杂，细微之旨也未尝具悉，行文粗阔简略，求其仿佛相近而已。
现更行撰录一篇，以补塞《参同契》的遗漏与脱落之处，并对文
义幽深之处进行润色，使之勾援连接而相逮及。此篇与《参同契》
是相等齐一的，其旨趣不相悖害，所以再次制作此篇，命之为
152
《五相类》。如此则大《易》之情性便能尽数得以体现了。
大《易》讲变化之道，以静为性，以动为情，动静情性各依
其法度而不相逾越。黄老之学顺应自然，穷究事物之根源，在养
生上相比较而言能提供可以运作与驾驭的方法。至于炉火炼丹之
事，此非无稽之言，是真乃有据可以稽考的事情。大《易》、黄老与
炉火三者，虽各由其径路以成就造化，而从道的意义上说，则皆
出于一门。
譬如果木，春天抽茎发枝，夏天布叶开花，秋天果实满枝垂
挂。大《易》、黄老、炉火发为三条，其发生之源则如同枝茎华叶
果出于根株而不失其本原之性一样，皆复归于道。我之所言完全
出自诚心，精细审思必有所成，不会贻误事绪
我是旧时国的一个鄙陋之人，隐居在深山幽谷之中，生命

申有开始之义。《左传》哀元年：“（少康）有田一成，有众一旅，能布其德，而
兆其谋，”基：本义为建筑物之脚基，引申亦有开始之义。《国语》晋九：“基于
其身，以克服其所。”注：“基，始也。”震圭因坤母得乾一阳而始成，故云“因
母立兆基”。
162
消息应钟律，升降据斗枢俞琰《周易参同契发挥》：“自子至已为息，
自午至亥为消。”息者，阳气之渐增也；消者，阳气之渐减也。阳气之渐增，从
复重至乾言，分别与律之黄钟、大吕、太簇、夹钟、姑洗、中吕相应。阳气之渐
减，从垢至坤，分别与律之宾、林钟、夷则、南吕、亡射、应钟相应。
《夏小正》有言：斗柄指东，天下皆春；斗柄指南，天下皆夏；斗柄指西，天下
皆秋；斗柄指北，天下皆冬。故复卦重应黄钟建子，临卦售应大吕建丑，泰卦
应太簇建寅，大壮卦应夹钟建卯，夫卦言应姑洗建辰，乾卦应中吕建已；垢
卦应药宾建午，遁卦应林钟建未，否卦应夷则建申，观卦重应南吕建酉，
剥卦重应亡射建戌，坤卦封应应钟建亥。
②此段言月相纳甲模型。所谓月相纳甲，系魏伯阳据京房八卦纳甲配月相所
成。初三日，月始萌，震王象之，纳庚，位在西方；初八日，月上弦，兑三象之，
纳丁，位在南方；十五日月望，乾三象之，纳甲，位在东方；十六日，月始缺，巽
象之，纳辛；二十三日，月下弦，艮三象之，纳丙，位在南方；三-十日，月晦，坤

29七八数十五，九六亦相当，四者合三十，易象索灭藏七八九六，在五行
数图式中居外围。七为火之成数，居南方；八为木之成数，居东方；九为金之成
数，居西方；六为水之成数，居北方。此四数之和为三十，与一月之日数相应。
三十日之数终，月相归于寂，八卦之象亦索然而灭藏。
163
佐阳诘商旅，人君深自藏语出《复·象》“先王以至日闭关，商旅不
行，后不省方”。至日闭关，指冬至日关闭商品交易市场。后不省方，指天子不
到诸侯国巡狩。后，即天子。方，即诸侯国。冬至为阴极阳生之时，宜静以养
阳，顺应天道。
③四象此处指七、八、九、六。七为少阳，八为少阴，九为老阳，六为老
阴，是为四象。四者之和恰为一月三十日之数。俞琰《周易参同契发挥》云：
“别序此七八九六之四象，以晓其未晓者。庶有以显夫一月三十日之数，皆譬喻，
而非真以月之三日进火，月之十六退符也。有如子午卯酉、子申寅戌、春夏秋
冬、分至启闭、昼夜晨昏、还返归居，皆此义也。学者若知七八九六即一月三十
日之数，则其余泛引曲喻，皆可默而识之矣，何必执文泥象哉！”此说是矣。后
文还取黄道上四方之恒星图像青龙（东方）、白虎（西方）、朱雀（南方）、玄武
（北方）为四象，以示炉火运作之情景。
3动则依卦变，静则循象辞据卦气说，六十四卦分节值时，故“循卦节”，

# Page 19

周易参回
即遵循节令时日，使人身之动与天时合节律。“象辞”，指卦辞。静则体悟卦辞吉
同契
区之理。
象内动，吉凶外起语出《易·系辞下》：“交象动乎内，吉凶见乎外，
功业见乎变，圣人之情见乎辞。”
五纬错顺，应时感动五纬，指水、火、木、金、土五颗行星，相对日月
为经而言。错顺，指视运动所得五星的运行状况。由于五星与地球都以太阳为同
心作圆周运动，且距太阳的远近各不相同。人在地球上观察五星运行，常有顺、
逆、留守之感。顺指自西向东，逆指自东向西，留守指在原地不动，这是视运动
必然导致的结果，并非真运动的本质体现。哥伯尼的太阳中心说正是从这里打开
了走向真运动的缺口，而中国传统天文学在这个问题上一直未能摆脱天人感应的
大中华文库
楷。所谓“应时感动”，谓政事明则五纬顺行，反之则或逆或留即错行。
四七乖戾，离仰俯“四七”，注家多以为指四方之七宿，合而为二十八
宿，可从。“四七乖戾”当指四方七宿现于天空之时与历法不吻合。东方七宿始
现为春，南方七宿始现为夏，西方七宿始现为秋，北方七宿始现为冬，此与前文
魁柄之说合。乖戾，指天象与历法背离，如依历法，立春以后，东方七宿应当开
始逐渐显现而未现。离：移，改也；离，迁也。所改所迁者，实由岁差现象造
成，历法当据新的观测数据修订。而汉代天文尚不知岁差，特别至东汉末七宿之
164
现常不应历，故有此说。“俯仰”，谓上下，指人事动于下，而天象变乎上，此亦
天人感应之说也。
6文昌统录，诘责台辅董德宁《周易参同契正义》：“文昌者，《史记·天
官书》云：‘斗魁戴匡六星，日文昌也。’统录者，谓文昌乃统录诸司之职，故
日‘文昌统录’也。洁，责也，问也。台辅者，《汉书·天文志》云：‘魁下六
星，两两而比者，日三台也；抱天枢四星，所以辅佐北极者，日四辅也。’盖台
台辅’也。然文昌即统录于其上，而台辅诘责于其下，自应阴阳燮理，鼎鼎调
和，则百官有司，乃典守其部，而各称其职位矣。”陈显微《周易参同契解》：
“丹居神室，犹人君之立国。而人君之立国，盖取于天象，有三台公辅之位，有
文昌统录之司。台辅之职，则坐而论道，调燮阴阳，使百官各尽其能。故统录在
文昌也，百官有司各称其职，则民物安妥而天下太平。众卦火符不失其度，则万
化流通而圣胎增长。然治国者在一人之所招，修丹者在一心之所感而已。”

40执法刺讥，诘过贻主此言君臣无道，天必降灾遣告之。朱元育《参同契
165
凶咎不可胜言矣。天有执法之星，主刺讥过失，即太微垣中左执法、右执法也。”
君主骄溢，亢满违道，当责其君；臣下邪倭，行不顺轨，当责其臣。故“贻主”
当指相关责任人，即相关主事之人。
4辰极处正，优游任下辰，北辰星也。极，处天之中也。《论语·为政》：
“为政以德，臂如北辰居其所而众星共之。”北辰，上帝之所。由于文昌、台辅皆
为帝之辅佐，故帝可以优游无为而臣下则各有所司。人间帝王，法天而行，居明
堂而行德政，则国泰民安，害道不行矣。
4内以养已，安静虚无按月相纳甲，坎戊离己居中间土位，故己为离中真
土。按五行五脏模型，脾居中间土位，牌土主意，故“养已”即调养心神，做到
无私无欲，安静虚无。

4一者以掩蔽，世人莫知之一者，指自初至终，丹道自然，一以贯之。俗
世之人，蔽于初终一贯之理，不重调神筑基于初始，故一无所成于终结。注家或
以为“一”指道，似与上下文意略有距离，但可备一参。
4上德无为，不以察求；下德为之，其用不休“上德无为”，“下德为之”，
大中华文库
语出《道德经》第三十八章“上德无为而无不为，下德为之而有以为”。魏公借
此以上德喻离火，以下德喻坎水。离火居上无为，坎水上行与离火相交，由此而
上下浮沉，敌云“其用不休”。此即五言句之两孔穴法、
4上闭则称有，下闭则称无“上闭”“下闭”云云，为戊已牌土意念之用。
“下闭”，则肾水之气上行，故其下“称无”，以其奉上也。“上闭”，则肾水与心
火交感，造化之神机存也，故“称有”。
166
《周易参同契发挥》：“此两孔穴法，有无亦相须者，下不闭则火不聚，上不闭则
药不升也。《悟真篇》云：‘送归土釜牢封闭，次人流珠厮配当。’故必相须而后
可也。何谓流珠土釜？即坎离两穴之异名也。”
50知白守黑，神明自来铅在白然状态下，表层被氧化成灰黑色，里层仍为
其本来的银白色。句谓把握了自然铅外黑里白的特征，则可知具有神奇功效的铅
丹一定可以炼制出来。“知白守黑”，语出《道德经》第二十八章：“知其白，守
其黑，为天下式。”
5水者道枢，其数名一，阴阳之始，玄含黄芽依据阴阳合气生成五行之
序，始生者为水，次火，次木，次金，次土，故五行之数水一、火二、木三、金
四、土五。水既然是阴阳造化之首创，故为一阴一阳之道的枢纽，蕴涵着万物生
成的根蒂。“黄芽”，即万物生成之根蒂，此根蒂是白然之丹产生的根据，因而也
是炉火炼制金丹的根据。

阳、五行、众类，故铅是天地之父母，阴阳之本元。盖圣人采天地父母之根而为
大药之基，聚阴阳纯粹之精，而为还丹之质，故殆非常物之造化也。”此说似嫌

5神气满室，莫之能留，守之者昌，失之者亡《管子·内业》：“有神自在

亡，关键在于无思无为，恪守虚静。
58是非历藏法，内视有所思历藏法，即咽津内视法。历来是非不定，伯阳
视其为邪道，即非修丹之正途。此法为每日清晨，依东南中西北之次审祝舌料，
舔上齿表、下齿表、内唇、漱口满而咽之。此法又称服五牙之气。行此法宜思人
其脏，使其液宣通各依所主。为东方人肝，南方人心，中央人脾，西方人肺，北
方人肾。此法虽不达于仙道，但于健身多有益，不可废。

64偃月作鼎炉，白虎为熬枢“偃月作鼎炉”，谓将真铅沿鼎壁铺设，故本或
云“法鼎炉”；真铅与鼎器之形相合，形为偃月。朱熹《周易参同契考异》：“偃
月，疑前下圆，后上缺，状如偃月也。”“白虎为熬枢”，白虎喻铅，谓进火之关

之“熬”，则指鼎器。下句谓汞为太阳之精物，流珠为汞日流动之象，青龙喻汞。

故日‘三性既会合，本性共宗祖’。初云夫妇，以两性相配而言也。继云父子，
言两性之所自出也，究云宗祖，乃并为一性矣。夫妇喻坎离，父母喻乾坤，是为

①土游于四季，守界定规矩依地支配月令，寅卯辰为春三个月，寅卯为

之境域，并依一定功法运行。脾土居中，以意领气，游于肾水冬，肺金秋，心火
夏、肝木春之间，循环往复，故云“土游于四季”，后世称此功法为小周天。

(在义设刑，当仁施德《易·系辞下》：“理财正辞，禁民为非，日义。”
意为：管理财物，要有正确的规章，并用相应的手段禁止民众违法乱纪地抢夺，
这就是义。《契》由此引|申为：设立刑罚以维护财物管理的正义性，因而属于义
的范围，刑德说是阴阳家学说的重要内容，《管子·四时》：“是故阴阳者，天地
之大理也。四时者，阴阳之大经也。刑德者，四时之合也。刑德合于时则生福，
诡则生祸。”因此主张秋冬用刑，以应阴气之肃杀；春夏用德，以应阳气之生长。
此说后为黄老之学吸收，汉代儒学亦有所取。《契》此言在于说明刑德之说与儒
家的仁义思想相一致。

阳燧以取火，非日不生光；方诸非星月，安能得水浆阳燧，凹面镜，又

大蛤或水晶珠为之，摩热后对着星月可以得水。陈显徽《抱一子解周易参同契》

生水取火：人身之中阴阳升降，与天地造化同运，其间水火交通之理，亦岂外乎
日月往来之机，以求证效哉！”

真人喻还丹，合于《契》旨。规中，为藏丹之所，是对深渊位置的补充交代，或

于中；闭塞目关，则神敛于中；闭塞口关，则气会于中。正以规中，乃真人深潜
Tong
之所，当守其浮游之气也。”仇氏视规中为真人深藏之所，得之，但未言其位置，
深以为憾。
为已之轴辖，动静不竭穷车轴，贯车轮并承载车厢者。辖，为固定车轮
与车轴位置而插人孔穴的销钉。没有辖，轮不能固定在车轴之上，从而不能有车
轮之用。故要使车轮动静不竭穷，把握辖的固定作用是其根本要义。

metals, a phenomenon in which later alchemists saw evidence of the
presence of mercury.

They did not love to live, nor did they loathe to die. And so they did
not die untimely deaths. They did not hold themselves dear and they

Good and evil did not bother their hearts. Mountains and peaks did not

with the same characteristic features, is described in the Speculum
Alchemiae.

creation of man, for, as the child is nourished at first with lighter

that the fire is to be augmented and made stronger gradually and in

Appendix
APpendix IⅡ:PREFACE OF THE 1932 EDITION
1932
The present translation of the Can Tong Qi ( 参同契） is believed
Edition
to be the first translation into a European language of the entire text of a
Chinese treatise on alchemy. Apart from its antiquity and its obvious
interest, it has the value for Occidental scholars that it supplies source
CLASSICS
material for study in a largely unexplored field of the history of
LIBRARY OFCHINI
science. This value will increase in the future. One portion of the Can
Tong Qi concatenates with others and illuminates them , until finally the
philosophical background of the doctrine of WEI BOYANG （ 魏白 阳)
can easily be made out. In the same way, other studies on Chinese
alchemy and an acquaintance with other treatises will later give us a
better understanding of the evolution and movement of alchemical ideas
and will enable us to guess their origin with greater probability. The
225
translation is based upon the version of YU YAN （ 俞 琰）who
compared the various versions of the Can Tong Qi which existed in his
time，includingthat of ZHUXI（朱熹）or ZHUFUZI（朱夫子），
prepared a critical compilation of variants entitled Can Tong Qi ( 参同]
契释疑， An Explanation of Doubtful Points of the Can Tong Qi），and
Can Tong Qi Fa Hui （参同契发挥，A Dissertation on the Can Tong
Qi). The commentary of YU YAN was found to be of assistance in the
preparation of the translation. We have divided the treatise arbitrarily
into different chapters from those of YU YAN. In the case of certain
obscure passages of YU YAN's version, where a study of the

# Page 31

附周
commentary still left it impossible to make a clear translation, the
alternative passage of one or the other of the variants was taken. We
have used the text with variants and commentary of YU YAN which are
译文序
printed in Vols. 625—627 of the Collected Daoist Classics, published
by the Commercial Press, Ltd. , Shanghai, China. Since these writings
have for centuries been treasured by the followers of the Daoist
religion, it seems reasonable to suppose that they are authentic, or, at
least , that they approximate as closely to their originals as do the texts,
say, of the Hebrew scriptures.
大中华文库
The translation of the Can Tong Qi and of the other material from
the Chinese to which reference is made or which is quoted in the
" Introduction" or the “ Notes" is entirely the work of WU LUQIANG
（吴 鲁 强）．ExceptforthematerialfromtheChinese，the

DAVIS. Each of us has read all of the manuscript, and each has
226

subject are not agreed, and occasionally it offers an interpretation of the
evidence. But the present translation of the Can Tong Qi is itself also
evidence which requires to be interpreted. Some of its indications are
pointed out in the “ Notes" , and others will be more apparent when other
alchemical texts are available. The “ Notes " are intended particularly for

ApPendix ⅢI :INTRODUCTION OF THE 1932 EDITION
译文前言
WEI BOYANG （ 魏伯 β阳） has been called the father of Chinese
alchemy. His Can Tong Qi ( 参[同] 契） is regarded as the earliest work
in the Chinese language which is devoted exclusively to the subject , but
it is by no means the earliest Chinese writing which makes mention of
alchemy. It shows that a definite and extended alchemical tradition
大中华文库
already existed, and mentions several earlier alchemists concerning
whom other information is available either from their own writings or
from the historical books.
For three or four centuries before WEI BOYANG, the Chinese
had been engaged in an effort to transmute base metals into gold—not
because gold was intrinsically valuable, but because the magical
228
efficacy of artificial gold was such that the eating of food out of vessels
prepare the elixir of immortality from cinnabar.  The similarity of the
methods and aims of the Chinese alchemists with those of the later
Greek, Arab, and Latin Europeans suggests that all alchemy derives
from a single origin. While it has been thought that the appearance of
alchemy in China in the second or third century B. C. resulted from the
influx of alchemistic teaching from Egypt or from Mesopotamia, or
possibly from India, we have no early alchemical documents from the
latter places—and the Can Tong Qi of WEI BOYANG appears to be the
earliest treatise entirely on alchemy with which we are at present
acquainted.

# Page 34

WEIBOYANG
WEI BOYANG was a Daoist philosopher and alchemist, a native
of Wu （吴）in the present province of Jiangsu （江苏).In the year
121 A. D. he was summoned to Court but refused the invitation because
of the 1
Qi he describes himself as “ a lowly man from the country of Kuai
Edition
(会), who has no love for worldly power, glory, fame, or gains, who
wastes his days leading a simple, quiet, leisurely, and peaceful life in a
CLASSICS
retreat in an unfrequented valley. "
LIBRARY OF CHINESE
The Can Tong Qi is one of the Daoist Classics and purports to be a
preparation of the pill of immortality. The Lie Xian Quan Zhuan ( 列J
仙 全 传，Complete Biographies of the Immortals ）says that WEI
BOYANG “ entered the mountains to make efficacious medicines. With
him were three disciples, two of whom he thought were lacking in
complete faith. When the medicine was made, he tested them. He
229
said, ° The gold medicine is made but it ought first to be tested on the
dog. If no harm comes to the dog, we may then take it ourselves; but
if the dog dies of it, we ought not to take it. ′ (Now BOYANG had
brought a white dog along with him to the mountain. If the number of
the treatments of the medicine had not been sufficient or if harmonious
compounding had not reached the required standard, it would contain a
little poison and would cause temporary death. ) BOYANG fed the
medicine to the dog, and the dog died an instantaneous death.
Whereupon he said, ° The medicine is not yet done. The dog has died
of it. Doesn't this show that the divine light has not been attained? If
we take it ourselves, I am afraid we shall go the same way as the dog.

o o's s  io
worldly route and forsaken my home to come here. I should be
ashamed to return if I could not attain the Xian ( ull, immortal). So,
to live without taking the medicine would be just the same as to die of
the medicine. I must take it.′ With these final words he put the
medicine into his mouth and died instantly. On seeing this , one of the
disciples said, ‘ Our teacher was no common person. He took the
medicine and died of it.  He must have done that with especial

cutter whom they met , he sent a letter of thanks to the two disciples. The
two disciples were filled with regret when they read the letter. " The Lie
Xian Quan Zhuan further states that WEI BOYANG wrote two treatises,

appear to be expositions of the Book of Change but in reality the diagrams

大中华文库
WU-XING ANDYIN-YANG
Chinese alchemy, like all of Chinese science, is erected upon the
fundamental concepts of Wu-xing ( 五行, the Five Elements） and yin-
yang （ 阴 阳， the Two Contraries ）．Both are genuine scientific
concepts which supply categories for the description of natural things,
and both in the writings of the alchemists are involved with magical and
fantastic connotations. An examination of their history① will throw light
232
on the origins of Chinese alchemy.
The notion of Wu-xing—the quintet: water, fire, wood, gold (or
metal) , and earth-—at first had no magical relationships. One of the
earliest cases of its use is in the Hong Fan ( 洪范， Great Plan） chapter
(twelfth century B.C.） of the Shu Jing （ Book of Historical
(鲸）， a deluge overran the Kingdom putting wu-xing at nought. "
LIANG, commenting on this passage, says that it ought to be
interpreted as meaning that on account of the deluge all the things

Although the term, wu-xing,  at first meant only the five

Edition
While the notion of wu-xing, in its original meaning of the five
elements, remounts to the twelfth century B. C. , the scientific notion
of yin-yang, or the two contrary principles, became definite at a later
CLASSICS
time, probably after CONFUCIUS (551 - 479 B. C. ), and had no
LIBRARYOFCHINESE
noteworthy importance until it appeared  in its fantastic and occult
connotations.
The word, yin, according to LIANG, originally had the meaning
of covering the sun with clouds. It came to mean shaded, dim,
beclouded, and, since Chinese cities were generally laid out with the
principal entrance on the southern side , it meant the northern or shaded
235
side of the city, also the south side of a river, the interior, the reverse ,
and the north side of a mountain. The word, yang, originally signified
oe s n na  ' ss n  sa g sdse nun  ,
the meaning of the brilliancy of the sun, hence sunlight, warmth, the
face-side , the exterior, the north side of a river, and the south side of a
city or of a mountain. The couplet, yin-yang, was used to signify
opposites, such as north and south, reverse and obverse, and this was
without magical connotation and without the belief that the opposites
were scientific categories by which the endless diversity of nature might
be understood and classified. The words, yin and yang, moreover,

CONFUCIUS are devoted to an exposition of that philosopher's
dualism. He conceived the universe as embracing two kinds of forces
which by interaction gave rise to the diverse facts of nature. The forces

some of the other pairs. In short, yin and yang, as used by
CONFUCIUS, had no magical connotations but did have, along with
other pairs of terms , a certain philosophical and scientific usefulness for
the discussion of natural phenomena.
The notion of yin-yang gradually became extended until in the
writings of the Daoists and alchemists it appears as a fundamental
cosmological concept having innumerable fantastic connotations. It was
supposed that the primal matter，taiji （太 极 ），in its gyrations
gradually separated into two parts, the liangyi （两 仪） or regulating
powers which together constituted the soul of the universe. The heavy
and gross part, yin, settled and formed the earth, while the fine and

light part, yang, remained suspended and formed the heavens. Yin was
the female principle, negative, heavy, earthy, and dry, typifying in
general the more undesirable aspects of nature such as cold, darkness,
weakness, and death. Yang was the positive, male principle,
of the

the universe were created and controlled in their various manifestations.
Yang was identified with the sun and yin with the moon. The

(i)  EDKINS says, loc. cit., Pp. 91 - 92, " The characteristics of early Daoism will help to
explain why the religionists of this school rapidly deteriorated in their aims and character, and

" The objects they proposed to themselves as the proper work of their religion, and the means
they chose to effect them, assumed more and more a physical aspect. They expected to be self-
reformed by the adoption of a physical regimen only. Not comprehending the immateriality and
immortality of the soul, they hoped it might be purged from its grossness and saved from decay by

activity, abstinence, a life free from excitement in some wild mountain retreat, that man could
looked to nature to provide a cure for the miseries of the world.
" In conformity with these views, many of them became hermits, and others sought for some
plant, or mineral, or compound preparation, by which exemption from the approaches of physical
corruption might be secured. Then followed the follies of alchemy. "
